========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I’m here because this has been going on for almost two years now, and I don’t feel like anyone has really listened properly. It’s about the kitchen work we had done—or were supposed to have done—and the contractor. I know how it sounds when you say it all at once, but I’m telling you, it isn’t just a simple mistake or poor workmanship.

The contractor, and I’m pretty sure some people on my block too, have been sort of quietly working together. Not openly, of course, that’s the whole point. But the kitchen has been left half-wrong on purpose, and every time I try to point it out, there’s some excuse or some little explanation that doesn’t add up. My husband keeps showing me receipts and saying, “Look, this was paid for, this was ordered,” things like that, but receipts don’t prove people aren’t cheating you. They just prove money changed hands.

It’s been nearly two years of this—excuses, delays, things not being right, people acting like I’m making too much of it. But I’m not. I know when something is being done deliberately, and this feels deliberate. That’s why I came in, because I’m tired of being treated like I don’t understand what’s happening in my own home.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s the pattern of it, doctor. I know people want one single piece of proof, like a letter where they all signed their names saying they’re cheating us, but that isn’t how people do things when they’re being sneaky.

The contractor would say one thing was ordered, then something else would show up, or it would be installed in a way that was just off enough that I’d notice but he could still shrug and say it was “within tolerance” or “that’s how it comes.” And every time I pushed back, there was another explanation. My husband keeps saying, “We have receipts, we paid for this, they delivered that,” but receipts don’t prove they did the job honestly. They don’t prove the kitchen wasn’t left half-wrong on purpose.

And then with people on the block, it’s not like they come up and say it directly. It’s more the way they act, the timing of things, the little looks, the way conversations stop. It feels coordinated. Like they know what’s going on and they’re part of making me look unreasonable when I’m just trying to get what we paid for.

I’ve been living with this for nearly two years now, and after that long, you start to see how all the pieces fit together. It’s not one mistake. It’s too many excuses, too many things wrong, too many people acting like I should just accept it. That’s how I know.
=====================================

It’s mostly this. I mean, I’m not saying every single person everywhere is out to get me, if that’s what you mean. I go to the store, I talk to people, and I don’t walk around thinking everyone is involved. But this kitchen situation, and the contractor, and some of the people on my block—yes, that’s where it is, and that’s what I’ve been trying to explain.

It started with the work in the kitchen, the things being wrong in ways that didn’t make sense, and then over time it became clear to me that it wasn’t just the contractor being careless. There are people nearby who seem to know about it, or act like they know, and they’re sort of helping make it seem like I’m the difficult one. And my husband keeps pointing to receipts like that settles it, but it doesn’t. A receipt doesn’t prove the cabinets weren’t done wrong on purpose or that the job wasn’t dragged out and twisted around to cheat us.

So no, I wouldn’t say it’s in every part of my life. It’s centered on this—on the kitchen, the contractor, and the way certain people on the block have been quietly involved. That’s been going on nearly two years, and after that long, you can tell when something is not just a misunderstanding.

I know how it sounds when you say it all at once, but I’m telling you, it isn’t just a simple mistake or poor workmanship.\n\nThe contractor, and I’m pretty sure some people on my block too, have been sort of quietly working together. Have you noticed any specific actions or proof that confirms your suspicions?\n\nPatient: It’s the pattern of it, doctor. I know people want one single piece of proof, like a letter where they all signed their names saying they’re cheating us, but that isn’t how people do things when they’re being sneaky.\n\nThe contractor would say one thing was ordered, then something else would show up, or it would be installed in a way that was just off enough that I’d notice but he could still shrug and say it was “within tolerance” or “that’s how it comes.” And every time I pushed back, there was another explanation. My husband keeps saying, “We have receipts, we paid for this, they delivered that,” but receipts don’t prove they did the job honestly. They don’t prove the kitchen wasn’t left half-wrong on purpose.\n\nAnd then with people on the block, it’s not like they come up and say it directly. It’s more the way they act, the timing of things, the little looks, the way conversations stop. It feels coordinated. Like they know what’s going on and they’re part of making me look unreasonable when I’m just trying to get what we paid for.\n\nI’ve been living with this for nearly two years now, and after that long, you start to see how all the pieces fit together. It’s not one mistake. It’s too many excuses, too many things wrong, too many people acting like I should just accept it. That’s how I know.\n\nDoctor: Do these kinds of experiences—feeling like people are working against you or trying to deceive you—happen in other parts of your life, or is this mostly about the kitchen and the contractor?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly this. I mean, I’m not saying every single person everywhere is out to get me, if that’s what you mean. I go to the store, I talk to people, and I don’t walk around thinking everyone is involved. But this kitchen situation, and the contractor, and some of the people on my block—yes, that’s where it is, and that’s what I’ve been trying to explain.\n\nIt started with the work in the kitchen, the things being wrong in ways that didn’t make sense, and then over time it became clear to me that it wasn’t just the contractor being careless. There are people nearby who seem to know about it, or act like they know, and they’re sort of helping make it seem like I’m the difficult one. And my husband keeps pointing to receipts like that settles it, but it doesn’t. A receipt doesn’t prove the cabinets weren’t done wrong on purpose or that the job wasn’t dragged out and twisted around to cheat us.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s in every part of my life. It’s centered on this—on the kitchen, the contractor, and the way certain people on the block have been quietly involved. That’s been going on nearly two years, and after that long, you can tell when something is not just a misunderstanding.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had similar kinds of suspicions or beliefs about other situations in your life, even before the kitchen work started?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Before the kitchen? No, not like this. I mean, I’ve had disagreements with people over the years like anyone else, and I’ve felt someone was being unfair or maybe not telling the whole truth, but nothing that stayed with me like this or had this many pieces to it.

This really started with the kitchen work. That’s what I keep trying to explain. It wasn’t that I was already going around suspecting people and then I just picked the contractor. It was the other way around—the work started going wrong, then the explanations didn’t match what I could see with my own eyes, and then it kept happening over and over. And after nearly two years of that, with the contractor giving excuses and my husband showing me receipts as if receipts prove nobody cheated us, it became obvious to me that something was being done on purpose.

So no, I don’t think I had this same kind of belief before. This is about the contractor, the kitchen being kept half-wrong, and some people on my block quietly being involved or at least knowing about it. That’s where it is.

Have you noticed any specific actions or proof that confirms your suspicions?\n\nPatient: It’s the pattern of it, doctor. I know people want one single piece of proof, like a letter where they all signed their names saying they’re cheating us, but that isn’t how people do things when they’re being sneaky.\n\nThe contractor would say one thing was ordered, then something else would show up, or it would be installed in a way that was just off enough that I’d notice but he could still shrug and say it was “within tolerance” or “that’s how it comes.” And every time I pushed back, there was another explanation. Like they know what’s going on and they’re part of making me look unreasonable when I’m just trying to get what we paid for.\n\nI’ve been living with this for nearly two years now, and after that long, you start to see how all the pieces fit together. There are people nearby who seem to know about it, or act like they know, and they’re sort of helping make it seem like I’m the difficult one. And my husband keeps pointing to receipts like that settles it, but it doesn’t. A receipt doesn’t prove the cabinets weren’t done wrong on purpose or that the job wasn’t dragged out and twisted around to cheat us.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s in every part of my life. It’s centered on this—on the kitchen, the contractor, and the way certain people on the block have been quietly involved. That’s been going on nearly two years, and after that long, you can tell when something is not just a misunderstanding.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had similar kinds of suspicions or beliefs about other situations in your life, even before the kitchen work started?\n\nPatient: Before the kitchen? No, not like this. I mean, I’ve had disagreements with people over the years like anyone else, and I’ve felt someone was being unfair or maybe not telling the whole truth, but nothing that stayed with me like this or had this many pieces to it.\n\nThis really started with the kitchen work. That’s what I keep trying to explain. It wasn’t that I was already going around suspecting people and then I just picked the contractor. It was the other way around—the work started going wrong, then the explanations didn’t match what I could see with my own eyes, and then it kept happening over and over. And after nearly two years of that, with the contractor giving excuses and my husband showing me receipts as if receipts prove nobody cheated us, it became obvious to me that something was being done on purpose.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I had this same kind of belief before. This is about the contractor, the kitchen being kept half-wrong, and some people on my block quietly being involved or at least knowing about it. That’s where it is.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had any beliefs or suspicions in the past that others turned out not to be true, but you were absolutely sure about at the time?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re asking. I’m trying to think back, because I understand why you’d ask that, but I can’t remember some other big situation where I was completely sure people were doing something and then later it turned out I’d imagined the whole thing.

This is different. This is why I’m here, really. For almost two years now, I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And I know my husband keeps showing me receipts, and they all have their explanations, but that doesn’t settle it for me. Receipts don’t show intention. They don’t show what was done deliberately or how people can cover things up while still making it look normal on paper.

So if you mean, have I had this happen before and then found out I was wrong—no, I can’t say that I have. This isn’t some pattern from my past. This is about what’s been happening with the kitchen, the contractor, and the people around here acting like they know more than they admit.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I suppose this situation would count, if that’s what you mean. My husband keeps acting like the receipts prove everything was done properly, or that because money changed hands and there are invoices, then nobody could have been cheating us. But that’s not proof to me. It’s just paperwork.

I’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And it doesn’t matter how many excuses they give, or how many times someone says, “Oh, that’s just how the work turned out,” because I’ve lived with it every day. I’ve seen the way things were left, and the way people talk around it instead of answering directly.

So yes, other people have shown me things they call evidence, mostly receipts and explanations, but I don’t accept that it proves I’m wrong. To me, it just shows they’re trying to make it look ordinary when it isn’t.

But that’s not proof to me. It’s just paperwork.\n\nI’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I wouldn’t say “without any clear evidence,” because to me the evidence is in what happened. But I know what you mean, I think. Other people act like there’s no clear evidence because it isn’t written down somewhere in black and white.

The main time is this kitchen situation. I’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And my husband keeps showing me receipts, like that settles it, like because there’s a receipt then nobody could have been deceiving us. But receipts don’t prove what someone intended. They don’t prove they weren’t cutting corners or making things look normal while doing something else underneath.

So if you’re asking whether I’ve felt someone was deliberately deceiving me, yes, I have. I still do. But I don’t accept that there’s “no evidence.” It’s just that the evidence is in the pattern, in the way the work was left, the excuses, the way people seem to know things and then act innocent. That’s what people don’t seem to want to hear.

That’s how I know.\n\nDoctor: Do these kinds of experiences—feeling like people are working against you or trying to deceive you—happen in other parts of your life, or is this mostly about the kitchen and the contractor?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly this. I mean, I’m not saying every single person everywhere is out to get me, if that’s what you mean. I go to the store, I talk to people, and I don’t walk around thinking everyone is involved. But this kitchen situation, and the contractor, and some of the people on my block—yes, that’s where it is, and that’s what I’ve been trying to explain.\n\nIt started with the work in the kitchen, the things being wrong in ways that didn’t make sense, and then over time it became clear to me that it wasn’t just the contractor being careless. There are people nearby who seem to know about it, or act like they know, and they’re sort of helping make it seem like I’m the difficult one. And my husband keeps pointing to receipts like that settles it, but it doesn’t. A receipt doesn’t prove the cabinets weren’t done wrong on purpose or that the job wasn’t dragged out and twisted around to cheat us.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s in every part of my life. It’s centered on this—on the kitchen, the contractor, and the way certain people on the block have been quietly involved. That’s been going on nearly two years, and after that long, you can tell when something is not just a misunderstanding.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had similar kinds of suspicions or beliefs about other situations in your life, even before the kitchen work started?\n\nPatient: Before the kitchen? No, not like this. I mean, I’ve had disagreements with people over the years like anyone else, and I’ve felt someone was being unfair or maybe not telling the whole truth, but nothing that stayed with me like this or had this many pieces to it.\n\nThis really started with the kitchen work. That’s what I keep trying to explain. It wasn’t that I was already going around suspecting people and then I just picked the contractor. It was the other way around—the work started going wrong, then the explanations didn’t match what I could see with my own eyes, and then it kept happening over and over. And after nearly two years of that, with the contractor giving excuses and my husband showing me receipts as if receipts prove nobody cheated us, it became obvious to me that something was being done on purpose.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I had this same kind of belief before. This is about the contractor, the kitchen being kept half-wrong, and some people on my block quietly being involved or at least knowing about it. That’s where it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or suspicions in the past that others turned out not to be true, but you were absolutely sure about at the time?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re asking. I’m trying to think back, because I understand why you’d ask that, but I can’t remember some other big situation where I was completely sure people were doing something and then later it turned out I’d imagined the whole thing.\n\nThis is different. This is why I’m here, really. For almost two years now, I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And I know my husband keeps showing me receipts, and they all have their explanations, but that doesn’t settle it for me. Receipts don’t show intention. They don’t show what was done deliberately or how people can cover things up while still making it look normal on paper.\n\nSo if you mean, have I had this happen before and then found out I was wrong—no, I can’t say that I have. This isn’t some pattern from my past. This is about what’s been happening with the kitchen, the contractor, and the people around here acting like they know more than they admit.\n\nDoctor: Have there ever been times when you believed something strongly, even when others showed you evidence that it wasn’t true?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose this situation would count, if that’s what you mean. My husband keeps acting like the receipts prove everything was done properly, or that because money changed hands and there are invoices, then nobody could have been cheating us. But that’s not proof to me. It’s just paperwork.\n\nI’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And it doesn’t matter how many excuses they give, or how many times someone says, “Oh, that’s just how the work turned out,” because I’ve lived with it every day. I’ve seen the way things were left, and the way people talk around it instead of answering directly.\n\nSo yes, other people have shown me things they call evidence, mostly receipts and explanations, but I don’t accept that it proves I’m wrong. To me, it just shows they’re trying to make it look ordinary when it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever, in your life, believed that someone was deliberately trying to deceive or harm you without any clear evidence to support that belief?\n\nPatient: Well… I wouldn’t say “without any clear evidence,” because to me the evidence is in what happened. But I know what you mean, I think. Other people act like there’s no clear evidence because it isn’t written down somewhere in black and white.\n\nThe main time is this kitchen situation. I’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And my husband keeps showing me receipts, like that settles it, like because there’s a receipt then nobody could have been deceiving us. But receipts don’t prove what someone intended. They don’t prove they weren’t cutting corners or making things look normal while doing something else underneath.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether I’ve felt someone was deliberately deceiving me, yes, I have. I still do. But I don’t accept that there’s “no evidence.” It’s just that the evidence is in the pattern, in the way the work was left, the excuses, the way people seem to know things and then act innocent. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know you keep saying “no real reason,” but that’s the part I can’t quite agree with. I understand that from the outside it may look like nothing happened, or like it’s just a kitchen job that went badly, but to me there have been reasons all along.

I’m here because for almost two years I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us, and to keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. Not just careless, not just a mistake here or there. It feels deliberate. The way things were left, the way explanations kept changing, the way people would act like I was making too much of it — that all means something to me.

And my husband keeps showing me receipts, saying, “Look, we paid for this, they did that,” as if receipts prove nobody was trying to deceive us. But receipts don’t show what people meant to do. They don’t show if someone was working with someone else, or if they were covering it up afterward. So, yes, I have believed people were trying to deceive me, but I don’t accept that there was no reason. Other people may not see it, but I do.

Not really outside of that, no. I mean, I’ve had worries like anyone, but nothing that took hold of me the way this has. This kitchen thing is the one I keep coming back to, because it isn’t just a thought I had one afternoon and then forgot.

For nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block were quietly working together to cheat us and keep the kitchen half-wrong on purpose. I know people want one single piece of proof, like a letter where they all signed their names saying they’re cheating us, but that isn’t how people do things when they’re being sneaky.\n\nThe contractor would say one thing was ordered, then something else would show up, or it would be installed in a way that was just off enough that I’d notice but he could still shrug and say it was “within tolerance” or “that’s how it comes.” And every time I pushed back, there was another explanation. My husband keeps saying, “We have receipts, we paid for this, they delivered that,” but receipts don’t prove they did the job honestly. They don’t prove the kitchen wasn’t left half-wrong on purpose.\n\nAnd then with people on the block, it’s not like they come up and say it directly. It’s more the way they act, the timing of things, the little looks, the way conversations stop. It feels coordinated. Like they know what’s going on and they’re part of making me look unreasonable when I’m just trying to get what we paid for.\n\nI’ve been living with this for nearly two years now, and after that long, you start to see how all the pieces fit together. It’s not one mistake. It’s too many excuses, too many things wrong, too many people acting like I should just accept it. That’s how I know.\n\nDoctor: Do these kinds of experiences—feeling like people are working against you or trying to deceive you—happen in other parts of your life, or is this mostly about the kitchen and the contractor?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly this. I mean, I’m not saying every single person everywhere is out to get me, if that’s what you mean. I go to the store, I talk to people, and I don’t walk around thinking everyone is involved. But this kitchen situation, and the contractor, and some of the people on my block—yes, that’s where it is, and that’s what I’ve been trying to explain.\n\nIt started with the work in the kitchen, the things being wrong in ways that didn’t make sense, and then over time it became clear to me that it wasn’t just the contractor being careless. There are people nearby who seem to know about it, or act like they know, and they’re sort of helping make it seem like I’m the difficult one. And my husband keeps pointing to receipts like that settles it, but it doesn’t. A receipt doesn’t prove the cabinets weren’t done wrong on purpose or that the job wasn’t dragged out and twisted around to cheat us.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s in every part of my life. It’s centered on this—on the kitchen, the contractor, and the way certain people on the block have been quietly involved. That’s been going on nearly two years, and after that long, you can tell when something is not just a misunderstanding.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had similar kinds of suspicions or beliefs about other situations in your life, even before the kitchen work started?\n\nPatient: Before the kitchen? No, not like this. I mean, I’ve had disagreements with people over the years like anyone else, and I’ve felt someone was being unfair or maybe not telling the whole truth, but nothing that stayed with me like this or had this many pieces to it.\n\nThis really started with the kitchen work. That’s what I keep trying to explain. It wasn’t that I was already going around suspecting people and then I just picked the contractor. It was the other way around—the work started going wrong, then the explanations didn’t match what I could see with my own eyes, and then it kept happening over and over. And after nearly two years of that, with the contractor giving excuses and my husband showing me receipts as if receipts prove nobody cheated us, it became obvious to me that something was being done on purpose.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I had this same kind of belief before. This is about the contractor, the kitchen being kept half-wrong, and some people on my block quietly being involved or at least knowing about it. That’s where it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or suspicions in the past that others turned out not to be true, but you were absolutely sure about at the time?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re asking. I’m trying to think back, because I understand why you’d ask that, but I can’t remember some other big situation where I was completely sure people were doing something and then later it turned out I’d imagined the whole thing.\n\nThis is different. This is why I’m here, really. For almost two years now, I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And I know my husband keeps showing me receipts, and they all have their explanations, but that doesn’t settle it for me. Receipts don’t show intention. They don’t show what was done deliberately or how people can cover things up while still making it look normal on paper.\n\nSo if you mean, have I had this happen before and then found out I was wrong—no, I can’t say that I have. This isn’t some pattern from my past. This is about what’s been happening with the kitchen, the contractor, and the people around here acting like they know more than they admit.\n\nDoctor: Have there ever been times when you believed something strongly, even when others showed you evidence that it wasn’t true?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose this situation would count, if that’s what you mean. My husband keeps acting like the receipts prove everything was done properly, or that because money changed hands and there are invoices, then nobody could have been cheating us. But that’s not proof to me. It’s just paperwork.\n\nI’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And it doesn’t matter how many excuses they give, or how many times someone says, “Oh, that’s just how the work turned out,” because I’ve lived with it every day. I’ve seen the way things were left, and the way people talk around it instead of answering directly.\n\nSo yes, other people have shown me things they call evidence, mostly receipts and explanations, but I don’t accept that it proves I’m wrong. To me, it just shows they’re trying to make it look ordinary when it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever, in your life, believed that someone was deliberately trying to deceive or harm you without any clear evidence to support that belief?\n\nPatient: Well… I wouldn’t say “without any clear evidence,” because to me the evidence is in what happened. But I know what you mean, I think. Other people act like there’s no clear evidence because it isn’t written down somewhere in black and white.\n\nThe main time is this kitchen situation. I’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And my husband keeps showing me receipts, like that settles it, like because there’s a receipt then nobody could have been deceiving us. But receipts don’t prove what someone intended. They don’t prove they weren’t cutting corners or making things look normal while doing something else underneath.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether I’ve felt someone was deliberately deceiving me, yes, I have. I still do. But I don’t accept that there’s “no evidence.” It’s just that the evidence is in the pattern, in the way the work was left, the excuses, the way people seem to know things and then act innocent. That’s what people don’t seem to want to hear.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you believed someone was trying to harm or deceive you, even though there was no real reason to think so or nothing actually happened to suggest that?\n\nPatient: I know you keep saying “no real reason,” but that’s the part I can’t quite agree with. I understand that from the outside it may look like nothing happened, or like it’s just a kitchen job that went badly, but to me there have been reasons all along.\n\nI’m here because for almost two years I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us, and to keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. Not just careless, not just a mistake here or there. It feels deliberate. The way things were left, the way explanations kept changing, the way people would act like I was making too much of it — that all means something to me.\n\nAnd my husband keeps showing me receipts, saying, “Look, we paid for this, they did that,” as if receipts prove nobody was trying to deceive us. But receipts don’t show what people meant to do. They don’t show if someone was working with someone else, or if they were covering it up afterward. So, yes, I have believed people were trying to deceive me, but I don’t accept that there was no reason. Other people may not see it, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a strong belief about something happening that others could not see or understand, even if it wasn’t related to the kitchen or the contractor?\n\nPatient: Not really outside of that, no. I mean, I’ve had worries like anyone, but nothing that took hold of me the way this has. This kitchen thing is the one I keep coming back to, because it isn’t just a thought I had one afternoon and then forgot.\n\nFor nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block were quietly working together to cheat us and keep the kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And I know other people say they don’t see it, or they act like I’m connecting things that aren’t connected, but to me it’s been very clear. My husband keeps showing me receipts, saying the money was paid and the work was done and that should settle it, but it doesn’t settle it for me. Receipts don’t explain why things were left the way they were or why everyone gives excuses instead of a straight answer.\n\nSo if you mean some other belief, no, I can’t think of another one like that. It’s this. This is the thing people don’t understand.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever, in your life, believed that someone was deliberately trying to deceive or harm you without any clear evidence to support that belief?\n\nPatient: Well… I wouldn’t say “without any clear evidence,” because to me the evidence is in what happened. But I know what you mean, I think. Other people act like there’s no clear evidence because it isn’t written down somewhere in black and white.\n\nThe main time is this kitchen situation. I’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And my husband keeps showing me receipts, like that settles it, like because there’s a receipt then nobody could have been deceiving us. But receipts don’t prove what someone intended. They don’t prove they weren’t cutting corners or making things look normal while doing something else underneath.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether I’ve felt someone was deliberately deceiving me, yes, I have. I still do. But I don’t accept that there’s “no evidence.” It’s just that the evidence is in the pattern, in the way the work was left, the excuses, the way people seem to know things and then act innocent. That’s what people don’t seem to want to hear.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you believed someone was trying to harm or deceive you, even though there was no real reason to think so or nothing actually happened to suggest that?\n\nPatient: I know you keep saying “no real reason,” but that’s the part I can’t quite agree with. I understand that from the outside it may look like nothing happened, or like it’s just a kitchen job that went badly, but to me there have been reasons all along.\n\nI’m here because for almost two years I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us, and to keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. Not just careless, not just a mistake here or there. It feels deliberate. The way things were left, the way explanations kept changing, the way people would act like I was making too much of it — that all means something to me.\n\nAnd my husband keeps showing me receipts, saying, “Look, we paid for this, they did that,” as if receipts prove nobody was trying to deceive us. I mean, I’ve had worries like anyone, but nothing that took hold of me the way this has. This kitchen thing is the one I keep coming back to, because it isn’t just a thought I had one afternoon and then forgot.\n\nFor nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block were quietly working together to cheat us and keep the kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And I know other people say they don’t see it, or they act like I’m connecting things that aren’t connected, but to me it’s been very clear. My husband keeps showing me receipts, saying the money was paid and the work was done and that should settle it, but it doesn’t settle it for me. Receipts don’t explain why things were left the way they were or why everyone gives excuses instead of a straight answer.\n\nSo if you mean some other belief, no, I can’t think of another one like that. It’s this. This is the thing people don’t understand.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period in your life when you felt suspicious or believed things others didn’t, even when there wasn’t a specific situation like the kitchen issue happening at the time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose, no matter how many receipts my husband shows me or how many excuses they give.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so, not in the same way. I’ve never been the kind of person who just walks around suspicious of everybody for no reason, if that’s what you mean. I raised my family, I worked hard when I was working, I dealt with neighbors, bills, all of that. I wasn’t sitting there inventing things.

This really started with the kitchen, and that’s why I keep coming back to it, because it’s not just a vague feeling. I’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And it doesn’t matter how many receipts my husband shows me, or how many excuses they give about why something was done this way or that way — those things don’t answer the real question for me.

So no, I wouldn’t say there was another period where I felt that way without something specific happening. It’s this specific situation. But with this situation, yes, I believe there’s more going on than everyone wants to admit.

They just prove money changed hands.\n\nIt’s been nearly two years of this—excuses, delays, things not being right, people acting like I’m making too much of it. But I’m not. I know when something is being done deliberately, and this feels deliberate. They don’t prove the kitchen wasn’t left half-wrong on purpose.\n\nAnd then with people on the block, it’s not like they come up and say it directly. It’s more the way they act, the timing of things, the little looks, the way conversations stop. It feels coordinated. Like they know what’s going on and they’re part of making me look unreasonable when I’m just trying to get what we paid for.\n\nI’ve been living with this for nearly two years now, and after that long, you start to see how all the pieces fit together. It’s not one mistake. It’s too many excuses, too many things wrong, too many people acting like I should just accept it. That’s how I know.\n\nDoctor: Do these kinds of experiences—feeling like people are working against you or trying to deceive you—happen in other parts of your life, or is this mostly about the kitchen and the contractor?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly this. And my husband keeps pointing to receipts like that settles it, but it doesn’t. That’s been going on nearly two years, and after that long, you can tell when something is not just a misunderstanding.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had similar kinds of suspicions or beliefs about other situations in your life, even before the kitchen work started?\n\nPatient: Before the kitchen? No, not like this. I mean, I’ve had disagreements with people over the years like anyone else, and I’ve felt someone was being unfair or maybe not telling the whole truth, but nothing that stayed with me like this or had this many pieces to it.\n\nThis really started with the kitchen work. And after nearly two years of that, with the contractor giving excuses and my husband showing me receipts as if receipts prove nobody cheated us, it became obvious to me that something was being done on purpose.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I had this same kind of belief before. This is about the contractor, the kitchen being kept half-wrong, and some people on my block quietly being involved or at least knowing about it. That’s where it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or suspicions in the past that others turned out not to be true, but you were absolutely sure about at the time?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re asking. I’m trying to think back, because I understand why you’d ask that, but I can’t remember some other big situation where I was completely sure people were doing something and then later it turned out I’d imagined the whole thing.\n\nThis is different. This is why I’m here, really. For almost two years now, I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And I know my husband keeps showing me receipts, and they all have their explanations, but that doesn’t settle it for me. Receipts don’t show intention. They don’t show what was done deliberately or how people can cover things up while still making it look normal on paper.\n\nSo if you mean, have I had this happen before and then found out I was wrong—no, I can’t say that I have. This isn’t some pattern from my past. This is about what’s been happening with the kitchen, the contractor, and the people around here acting like they know more than they admit.\n\nDoctor: Have there ever been times when you believed something strongly, even when others showed you evidence that it wasn’t true?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose this situation would count, if that’s what you mean. My husband keeps acting like the receipts prove everything was done properly, or that because money changed hands and there are invoices, then nobody could have been cheating us. It’s just paperwork.\n\nI’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. No, not really — not apart from the kitchen and the contractor. That’s what I keep trying to explain. People make it sound like I’m suspicious in general, like I just go around thinking everyone is out to get me, but that isn’t how it is.

This is about what happened with the kitchen. This is about what’s been happening with the kitchen, the contractor, and the people around here acting like they know more than they admit.\n\nDoctor: Have there ever been times when you believed something strongly, even when others showed you evidence that it wasn’t true?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose this situation would count, if that’s what you mean. But with this situation, I still believe there’s more to it than everyone is admitting.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had strong, unshakable beliefs about something happening that others could not see or confirm, even when there was no real-life situation—like the kitchen issue—that seemed to trigger it?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose, no matter how many receipts my husband shows me or how many excuses they give.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think there was something just out of nowhere, not like that. I mean, I know you keep asking whether I’ve had some belief that wasn’t tied to anything real going on, and I understand why you’re asking, but that’s not how this feels to me.

For me it’s been the kitchen. That’s the thing. I’m here because for almost two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block were quietly working together to cheat us and keep the kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And people say, “Well, where’s the proof?” or my husband shows me receipts again, like the receipts are supposed to make it all disappear, but they don’t. They don’t explain the way things were left, or why there were always excuses, or why it felt like people knew more than they were saying.

So if you mean did I ever have that kind of unshakable belief with no real-life situation at all behind it, I’d say no. This came from what happened with the contractor and the kitchen. But once it started, yes, I haven’t been able to let it go, because to me it still doesn’t add up.

Can you tell me what’s been bringing you here today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here because this has been going on for almost two years now, and I don’t feel like anyone has really listened properly. It’s about the kitchen work we had done—or were supposed to have done—and the contractor. I know how it sounds when you say it all at once, but I’m telling you, it isn’t just a simple mistake or poor workmanship.\n\nThe contractor, and I’m pretty sure some people on my block too, have been sort of quietly working together. Not openly, of course, that’s the whole point. But the kitchen has been left half-wrong on purpose, and every time I try to point it out, there’s some excuse or some little explanation that doesn’t add up. My husband keeps showing me receipts and saying, “Look, this was paid for, this was ordered,” things like that, but receipts don’t prove people aren’t cheating you. They just prove money changed hands.\n\nIt’s been nearly two years of this—excuses, delays, things not being right, people acting like I’m making too much of it. But I’m not. I know when something is being done deliberately, and this feels deliberate. That’s why I came in, because I’m tired of being treated like I don’t understand what’s happening in my own home.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you know the contractor and people on your block are working against you? Have you noticed any specific actions or proof that confirms your suspicions?\n\nPatient: It’s the pattern of it, doctor. I know people want one single piece of proof, like a letter where they all signed their names saying they’re cheating us, but that isn’t how people do things when they’re being sneaky.\n\nThe contractor would say one thing was ordered, then something else would show up, or it would be installed in a way that was just off enough that I’d notice but he could still shrug and say it was “within tolerance” or “that’s how it comes.” And every time I pushed back, there was another explanation. My husband keeps saying, “We have receipts, we paid for this, they delivered that,” but receipts don’t prove they did the job honestly. They don’t prove the kitchen wasn’t left half-wrong on purpose.\n\nAnd then with people on the block, it’s not like they come up and say it directly. It’s more the way they act, the timing of things, the little looks, the way conversations stop. It feels coordinated. Like they know what’s going on and they’re part of making me look unreasonable when I’m just trying to get what we paid for.\n\nI’ve been living with this for nearly two years now, and after that long, you start to see how all the pieces fit together. It’s not one mistake. It’s too many excuses, too many things wrong, too many people acting like I should just accept it. That’s how I know.\n\nDoctor: Do these kinds of experiences—feeling like people are working against you or trying to deceive you—happen in other parts of your life, or is this mostly about the kitchen and the contractor?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly this. I mean, I’m not saying every single person everywhere is out to get me, if that’s what you mean. I go to the store, I talk to people, and I don’t walk around thinking everyone is involved. But this kitchen situation, and the contractor, and some of the people on my block—yes, that’s where it is, and that’s what I’ve been trying to explain.\n\nIt started with the work in the kitchen, the things being wrong in ways that didn’t make sense, and then over time it became clear to me that it wasn’t just the contractor being careless. There are people nearby who seem to know about it, or act like they know, and they’re sort of helping make it seem like I’m the difficult one. And my husband keeps pointing to receipts like that settles it, but it doesn’t. A receipt doesn’t prove the cabinets weren’t done wrong on purpose or that the job wasn’t dragged out and twisted around to cheat us.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s in every part of my life. It’s centered on this—on the kitchen, the contractor, and the way certain people on the block have been quietly involved. That’s been going on nearly two years, and after that long, you can tell when something is not just a misunderstanding.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had similar kinds of suspicions or beliefs about other situations in your life, even before the kitchen work started?\n\nPatient: Before the kitchen? No, not like this. I mean, I’ve had disagreements with people over the years like anyone else, and I’ve felt someone was being unfair or maybe not telling the whole truth, but nothing that stayed with me like this or had this many pieces to it.\n\nThis really started with the kitchen work. That’s what I keep trying to explain. It wasn’t that I was already going around suspecting people and then I just picked the contractor. It was the other way around—the work started going wrong, then the explanations didn’t match what I could see with my own eyes, and then it kept happening over and over. And after nearly two years of that, with the contractor giving excuses and my husband showing me receipts as if receipts prove nobody cheated us, it became obvious to me that something was being done on purpose.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I had this same kind of belief before. This is about the contractor, the kitchen being kept half-wrong, and some people on my block quietly being involved or at least knowing about it. That’s where it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or suspicions in the past that others turned out not to be true, but you were absolutely sure about at the time?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re asking. I’m trying to think back, because I understand why you’d ask that, but I can’t remember some other big situation where I was completely sure people were doing something and then later it turned out I’d imagined the whole thing.\n\nThis is different. This is why I’m here, really. For almost two years now, I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And I know my husband keeps showing me receipts, and they all have their explanations, but that doesn’t settle it for me. Receipts don’t show intention. They don’t show what was done deliberately or how people can cover things up while still making it look normal on paper.\n\nSo if you mean, have I had this happen before and then found out I was wrong—no, I can’t say that I have. This isn’t some pattern from my past. This is about what’s been happening with the kitchen, the contractor, and the people around here acting like they know more than they admit.\n\nDoctor: Have there ever been times when you believed something strongly, even when others showed you evidence that it wasn’t true?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose this situation would count, if that’s what you mean. My husband keeps acting like the receipts prove everything was done properly, or that because money changed hands and there are invoices, then nobody could have been cheating us. But that’s not proof to me. It’s just paperwork.\n\nI’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And it doesn’t matter how many excuses they give, or how many times someone says, “Oh, that’s just how the work turned out,” because I’ve lived with it every day. I’ve seen the way things were left, and the way people talk around it instead of answering directly.\n\nSo yes, other people have shown me things they call evidence, mostly receipts and explanations, but I don’t accept that it proves I’m wrong. To me, it just shows they’re trying to make it look ordinary when it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever, in your life, believed that someone was deliberately trying to deceive or harm you without any clear evidence to support that belief?\n\nPatient: Well… I wouldn’t say “without any clear evidence,” because to me the evidence is in what happened. But I know what you mean, I think. Other people act like there’s no clear evidence because it isn’t written down somewhere in black and white.\n\nThe main time is this kitchen situation. I’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And my husband keeps showing me receipts, like that settles it, like because there’s a receipt then nobody could have been deceiving us. But receipts don’t prove what someone intended. They don’t prove they weren’t cutting corners or making things look normal while doing something else underneath.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether I’ve felt someone was deliberately deceiving me, yes, I have. I still do. But I don’t accept that there’s “no evidence.” It’s just that the evidence is in the pattern, in the way the work was left, the excuses, the way people seem to know things and then act innocent. That’s what people don’t seem to want to hear.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you believed someone was trying to harm or deceive you, even though there was no real reason to think so or nothing actually happened to suggest that?\n\nPatient: I know you keep saying “no real reason,” but that’s the part I can’t quite agree with. I understand that from the outside it may look like nothing happened, or like it’s just a kitchen job that went badly, but to me there have been reasons all along.\n\nI’m here because for almost two years I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us, and to keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. Not just careless, not just a mistake here or there. It feels deliberate. The way things were left, the way explanations kept changing, the way people would act like I was making too much of it — that all means something to me.\n\nAnd my husband keeps showing me receipts, saying, “Look, we paid for this, they did that,” as if receipts prove nobody was trying to deceive us. But receipts don’t show what people meant to do. They don’t show if someone was working with someone else, or if they were covering it up afterward. So, yes, I have believed people were trying to deceive me, but I don’t accept that there was no reason. Other people may not see it, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a strong belief about something happening that others could not see or understand, even if it wasn’t related to the kitchen or the contractor?\n\nPatient: Not really outside of that, no. I mean, I’ve had worries like anyone, but nothing that took hold of me the way this has. This kitchen thing is the one I keep coming back to, because it isn’t just a thought I had one afternoon and then forgot.\n\nFor nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block were quietly working together to cheat us and keep the kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And I know other people say they don’t see it, or they act like I’m connecting things that aren’t connected, but to me it’s been very clear. My husband keeps showing me receipts, saying the money was paid and the work was done and that should settle it, but it doesn’t settle it for me. Receipts don’t explain why things were left the way they were or why everyone gives excuses instead of a straight answer.\n\nSo if you mean some other belief, no, I can’t think of another one like that. It’s this. This is the thing people don’t understand.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life when you felt suspicious or believed things others didn’t, even when there wasn’t a specific situation like the kitchen issue happening at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the same way. I’ve never been the kind of person who just walks around suspicious of everybody for no reason, if that’s what you mean. I raised my family, I worked hard when I was working, I dealt with neighbors, bills, all of that. I wasn’t sitting there inventing things.\n\nThis really started with the kitchen, and that’s why I keep coming back to it, because it’s not just a vague feeling. I’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And it doesn’t matter how many receipts my husband shows me, or how many excuses they give about why something was done this way or that way — those things don’t answer the real question for me.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say there was another period where I felt that way without something specific happening. It’s this specific situation. But with this situation, yes, I believe there’s more going on than everyone wants to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs or suspicions that others could not understand or confirm, even when there was no clear evidence to support them, and these beliefs were not related to the kitchen work or the contractor?\n\nPatient: No, not really — not apart from the kitchen and the contractor. That’s what I keep trying to explain. People make it sound like I’m suspicious in general, like I just go around thinking everyone is out to get me, but that isn’t how it is.\n\nThis is about what happened with the kitchen. I’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And I know my husband says, “Here are the receipts, here’s what we paid, here’s what they said they did,” but that doesn’t settle it for me. Receipts don’t prove what was really going on behind the scenes. And all those excuses — why this was done badly, why that wasn’t fixed right, why people acted strange about it — they just don’t add up.\n\nSo if you mean some other belief, some separate thing that people couldn’t confirm, no, I can’t think of one. It’s this situation. But with this situation, I still believe there’s more to it than everyone is admitting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had strong, unshakable beliefs about something happening that others could not see or confirm, even when there was no real-life situation—like the kitchen issue—that seemed to trigger it?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there was something just out of nowhere, not like that. I mean, I know you keep asking whether I’ve had some belief that wasn’t tied to anything real going on, and I understand why you’re asking, but that’s not how this feels to me.\n\nFor me it’s been the kitchen. That’s the thing. Other people may not see it, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a strong belief about something happening that others could not see or understand, even if it wasn’t related to the kitchen or the contractor?\n\nPatient: Not really outside of that, no. No, I wouldn’t say there was something with no real-life situation at all. That’s the part I keep trying to separate, because it makes it sound like I just got an idea in my head for no reason and then refused to let it go, and that isn’t how it happened.

I’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And the excuses they give — why something wasn’t finished right, why something was delayed, why people suddenly acted strange — none of that has satisfied me.

So if you mean some belief that came out of nowhere, no. But if you mean have I held onto this strongly even when other people tell me I’m wrong, then yes, I have. Because to me it still feels like there’s more going on than they’re admitting.

I know when something is being done deliberately, and this feels deliberate. That’s why I came in, because I’m tired of being treated like I don’t understand what’s happening in my own home.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you know the contractor and people on your block are working against you? Have you noticed any specific actions or proof that confirms your suspicions?\n\nPatient: It’s the pattern of it, doctor. I know people want one single piece of proof, like a letter where they all signed their names saying they’re cheating us, but that isn’t how people do things when they’re being sneaky.\n\nThe contractor would say one thing was ordered, then something else would show up, or it would be installed in a way that was just off enough that I’d notice but he could still shrug and say it was “within tolerance” or “that’s how it comes.” And every time I pushed back, there was another explanation. My husband keeps saying, “We have receipts, we paid for this, they delivered that,” but receipts don’t prove they did the job honestly. They don’t prove the kitchen wasn’t left half-wrong on purpose.\n\nAnd then with people on the block, it’s not like they come up and say it directly. It’s more the way they act, the timing of things, the little looks, the way conversations stop. It feels coordinated. Like they know what’s going on and they’re part of making me look unreasonable when I’m just trying to get what we paid for.\n\nI’ve been living with this for nearly two years now, and after that long, you start to see how all the pieces fit together. It’s not one mistake. It’s too many excuses, too many things wrong, too many people acting like I should just accept it. That’s how I know.\n\nDoctor: Do these kinds of experiences—feeling like people are working against you or trying to deceive you—happen in other parts of your life, or is this mostly about the kitchen and the contractor?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly this. I mean, I’m not saying every single person everywhere is out to get me, if that’s what you mean. I go to the store, I talk to people, and I don’t walk around thinking everyone is involved. But this kitchen situation, and the contractor, and some of the people on my block—yes, that’s where it is, and that’s what I’ve been trying to explain.\n\nIt started with the work in the kitchen, the things being wrong in ways that didn’t make sense, and then over time it became clear to me that it wasn’t just the contractor being careless. There are people nearby who seem to know about it, or act like they know, and they’re sort of helping make it seem like I’m the difficult one. And my husband keeps pointing to receipts like that settles it, but it doesn’t. A receipt doesn’t prove the cabinets weren’t done wrong on purpose or that the job wasn’t dragged out and twisted around to cheat us.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s in every part of my life. It’s centered on this—on the kitchen, the contractor, and the way certain people on the block have been quietly involved. That’s been going on nearly two years, and after that long, you can tell when something is not just a misunderstanding.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had similar kinds of suspicions or beliefs about other situations in your life, even before the kitchen work started?\n\nPatient: Before the kitchen? No, not like this. I mean, I’ve had disagreements with people over the years like anyone else, and I’ve felt someone was being unfair or maybe not telling the whole truth, but nothing that stayed with me like this or had this many pieces to it.\n\nThis really started with the kitchen work. That’s what I keep trying to explain. It wasn’t that I was already going around suspecting people and then I just picked the contractor. It was the other way around—the work started going wrong, then the explanations didn’t match what I could see with my own eyes, and then it kept happening over and over. And after nearly two years of that, with the contractor giving excuses and my husband showing me receipts as if receipts prove nobody cheated us, it became obvious to me that something was being done on purpose.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I had this same kind of belief before. This is about the contractor, the kitchen being kept half-wrong, and some people on my block quietly being involved or at least knowing about it. That’s where it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or suspicions in the past that others turned out not to be true, but you were absolutely sure about at the time?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re asking. I’m trying to think back, because I understand why you’d ask that, but I can’t remember some other big situation where I was completely sure people were doing something and then later it turned out I’d imagined the whole thing.\n\nThis is different. This is why I’m here, really. For almost two years now, I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And I know my husband keeps showing me receipts, and they all have their explanations, but that doesn’t settle it for me. Receipts don’t show intention. They don’t show what was done deliberately or how people can cover things up while still making it look normal on paper.\n\nSo if you mean, have I had this happen before and then found out I was wrong—no, I can’t say that I have. This isn’t some pattern from my past. This is about what’s been happening with the kitchen, the contractor, and the people around here acting like they know more than they admit.\n\nDoctor: Have there ever been times when you believed something strongly, even when others showed you evidence that it wasn’t true?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose this situation would count, if that’s what you mean. My husband keeps acting like the receipts prove everything was done properly, or that because money changed hands and there are invoices, then nobody could have been cheating us. But that’s not proof to me. It’s just paperwork.\n\nI’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And it doesn’t matter how many excuses they give, or how many times someone says, “Oh, that’s just how the work turned out,” because I’ve lived with it every day. I’ve seen the way things were left, and the way people talk around it instead of answering directly.\n\nSo yes, other people have shown me things they call evidence, mostly receipts and explanations, but I don’t accept that it proves I’m wrong. To me, it just shows they’re trying to make it look ordinary when it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever, in your life, believed that someone was deliberately trying to deceive or harm you without any clear evidence to support that belief?\n\nPatient: Well… I wouldn’t say “without any clear evidence,” because to me the evidence is in what happened. But I know what you mean, I think. Other people act like there’s no clear evidence because it isn’t written down somewhere in black and white.\n\nThe main time is this kitchen situation. I’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And my husband keeps showing me receipts, like that settles it, like because there’s a receipt then nobody could have been deceiving us. But receipts don’t prove what someone intended. They don’t prove they weren’t cutting corners or making things look normal while doing something else underneath.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether I’ve felt someone was deliberately deceiving me, yes, I have. I still do. But I don’t accept that there’s “no evidence.” It’s just that the evidence is in the pattern, in the way the work was left, the excuses, the way people seem to know things and then act innocent. That’s what people don’t seem to want to hear.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you believed someone was trying to harm or deceive you, even though there was no real reason to think so or nothing actually happened to suggest that?\n\nPatient: I know you keep saying “no real reason,” but that’s the part I can’t quite agree with. I understand that from the outside it may look like nothing happened, or like it’s just a kitchen job that went badly, but to me there have been reasons all along.\n\nI’m here because for almost two years I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us, and to keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. Not just careless, not just a mistake here or there. It feels deliberate. The way things were left, the way explanations kept changing, the way people would act like I was making too much of it — that all means something to me.\n\nAnd my husband keeps showing me receipts, saying, “Look, we paid for this, they did that,” as if receipts prove nobody was trying to deceive us. But receipts don’t show what people meant to do. They don’t show if someone was working with someone else, or if they were covering it up afterward. So, yes, I have believed people were trying to deceive me, but I don’t accept that there was no reason. Other people may not see it, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a strong belief about something happening that others could not see or understand, even if it wasn’t related to the kitchen or the contractor?\n\nPatient: Not really outside of that, no. I mean, I’ve had worries like anyone, but nothing that took hold of me the way this has. This kitchen thing is the one I keep coming back to, because it isn’t just a thought I had one afternoon and then forgot.\n\nFor nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block were quietly working together to cheat us and keep the kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And I know other people say they don’t see it, or they act like I’m connecting things that aren’t connected, but to me it’s been very clear. My husband keeps showing me receipts, saying the money was paid and the work was done and that should settle it, but it doesn’t settle it for me. Receipts don’t explain why things were left the way they were or why everyone gives excuses instead of a straight answer.\n\nSo if you mean some other belief, no, I can’t think of another one like that. It’s this. This is the thing people don’t understand.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life when you felt suspicious or believed things others didn’t, even when there wasn’t a specific situation like the kitchen issue happening at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the same way. I’ve never been the kind of person who just walks around suspicious of everybody for no reason, if that’s what you mean. I raised my family, I worked hard when I was working, I dealt with neighbors, bills, all of that. I wasn’t sitting there inventing things.\n\nThis really started with the kitchen, and that’s why I keep coming back to it, because it’s not just a vague feeling. I’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. And it doesn’t matter how many receipts my husband shows me, or how many excuses they give about why something was done this way or that way — those things don’t answer the real question for me.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say there was another period where I felt that way without something specific happening. It’s this specific situation. But with this situation, yes, I believe there’s more going on than everyone wants to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs or suspicions that others could not understand or confirm, even when there was no clear evidence to support them, and these beliefs were not related to the kitchen work or the contractor?\n\nPatient: No, not really — not apart from the kitchen and the contractor. That’s what I keep trying to explain. People make it sound like I’m suspicious in general, like I just go around thinking everyone is out to get me, but that isn’t how it is.\n\nThis is about what happened with the kitchen. I’m here because for nearly two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us and keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. Receipts don’t prove what was really going on behind the scenes. That’s the thing. I’m here because for almost two years now I’ve been sure the contractor and some people on my block were quietly working together to cheat us and keep the kitchen half-wrong on purpose. This came from what happened with the contractor and the kitchen. But once it started, yes, I haven’t been able to let it go, because to me it still doesn’t add up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that you held strongly and unshakably, even when there was no real-life situation to support it at all and others clearly showed you that it wasn’t true?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say there was something with no real-life situation at all. No, not completely unrelated. I know I’ve said that a few times, but it’s important because people keep acting like I have all sorts of strange beliefs floating around, and I don’t. It’s this one situation.
